   > "He tells us to worship God alone... and he orders us to pray, be truthful, remain chaste, and keep family ties." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 7[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Khadija knew the private man before there was a public prophetic mission. At the first revelation, she reassured Prophet Muhammad ﷺ by appealing to years of conduct she had already witnessed and concluded that God would not disgrace a man who lived that way.

   > "You keep family ties, support the weak, help the destitute, honor the guest, and assist those struck by calamity." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Anas provides a decade of close observation from inside Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ household. A manufactured public persona is harder to sustain in daily life with a young household servant. Anas did not remember petty domination or constant irritation.

   > "I served the Prophet ﷺ for ten years. He never said 'uff' [an expression of irritation] to me." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 6038[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Aisha provides another intimate test: she said that he never struck a woman or a servant.

   > "The Messenger of God ﷺ never struck anyone with his hand, neither a woman nor a servant." — *Sahih Muslim* 2328a[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. For years, the claim brought Prophet Muhammad ﷺ loss rather than political power. The Meccan mission meant hostility, insecurity, persecution of his followers, loss of protection, rejection at al-Ta’if, and finally migration. A theory based on simple short-term personal gain fits this phase poorly.

   > "If you do not aid the Prophet - Allah has already aided him when those who disbelieved had driven him out [of Makkah] as one of two, when they were in the cave and he said to his companion, 'Do not grieve; indeed Allah is with us.' And Allah sent down his tranquillity upon him and supported him with angels you did not see and made the word of those who disbelieved the lowest, while the word of Allah - that is the highest. And Allah is Exalted in Might and Wise." (**Sūrat al-Tawbah 9:40**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿إِلَّا تَنصُرُوهُ فَقَدْ نَصَرَهُ ٱللَّهُ إِذْ أَخْرَجَهُ ٱلَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا۟ ثَانِىَ ٱثْنَيْنِ إِذْ هُمَا فِى ٱلْغَارِ إِذْ يَقُولُ لِصَـٰحِبِهِۦ لَا تَحْزَنْ إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ مَعَنَا ۖ فَأَنزَلَ ٱللَّهُ سَكِينَتَهُۥ عَلَيْهِ وَأَيَّدَهُۥ بِجُنُودٍ لَّمْ تَرَوْهَا وَجَعَلَ كَلِمَةَ ٱلَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا۟ ٱلسُّفْلَىٰ ۗ وَكَلِمَةُ ٱللَّهِ هِىَ ٱلْعُلْيَا ۗ وَٱللَّهُ عَزِيزٌ حَكِيمٌ ۝٤٠﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ التَّوۡبَةِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. When political power finally came, his household did not turn into a royal court. By the end of the mission Prophet Muhammad ﷺ led most of Arabia, yet personally owned remarkably little and built no palace. Aisha’s memory of months without cooking fires and the report that his armor remained pledged for food are difficult to fit to a theory centered on personal enrichment.

   > "The Messenger of God ﷺ died while his iron armor was pledged to a Jewish man for thirty measures of barley." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2916[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ closed off obvious routes by which his family might turn prophethood into dynastic wealth. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ taught that what prophets leave behind becomes charity rather than inherited family property, and his household was barred from receiving obligatory alms. When his young grandson Hasan put a charity date in his mouth, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ made him remove it. All of Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ sons died in childhood, and he made no arrangement to place another relative on a hereditary throne.

   > "We prophets are not inherited from. What we leave is charity." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4240-4241[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; *Sahih al-Bukhari* 1491[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; *Sahih Muslim* 1072a[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ explicitly forbade his followers from exaggerating his religious status. A founder seeking to maximize his own sacral authority would have every incentive to encourage such praise. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ instead restricted it.

   > "Do not exaggerate in praising me... I am only a servant. Say: God’s servant and His Messenger." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3445[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ denied possessing independent knowledge of the unseen.

   > "I hold not for myself [the power of] benefit or harm, except what Allah has willed. And if I knew the unseen, I could have acquired much wealth, and no harm would have touched me. I am not except a warner and a bringer of good tidings to a people who believe." (**Sūrat al-Aʿrāf 7:188**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿قُل لَّآ أَمْلِكُ لِنَفْسِى نَفْعًا وَلَا ضَرًّا إِلَّا مَا شَآءَ ٱللَّهُ ۚ وَلَوْ كُنتُ أَعْلَمُ ٱلْغَيْبَ لَٱسْتَكْثَرْتُ مِنَ ٱلْخَيْرِ وَمَا مَسَّنِىَ ٱلسُّوٓءُ ۚ إِنْ أَنَا۠ إِلَّا نَذِيرٌ وَبَشِيرٌ لِّقَوْمٍ يُؤْمِنُونَ ۝١٨٨﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الأَعۡرَافِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ denied possessing God’s treasures and denied being an angel.

   > "I do not say to you that I possess Allah’s treasuries or know the unseen, nor do I claim to be an angel. I only follow what is revealed to me." (**Sūrat al-Anʿām 6:50**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿قُل لَّآ أَقُولُ لَكُمْ عِندِى خَزَآئِنُ ٱللَّهِ وَلَآ أَعْلَمُ ٱلْغَيْبَ وَلَآ أَقُولُ لَكُمْ إِنِّى مَلَكٌ ۖ إِنْ أَتَّبِعُ إِلَّا مَا يُوحَىٰٓ إِلَىَّ ۚ قُلْ هَلْ يَسْتَوِى ٱلْأَعْمَىٰ وَٱلْبَصِيرُ ۚ أَفَلَا تَتَفَكَّرُونَ ۝٥٠﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الأَنۡعَامِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ rejected a remarkably convenient miracle claim when others supplied it for him. A solar eclipse occurred on the day his son Ibrahim died, and people linked the two events. For a fraudulent religious leader, the coincidence would have been exceptionally useful. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ immediately denied that the eclipse had anything to do with his son’s death.

   > "The sun and moon do not eclipse because of anyone’s death or life." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 1043[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. At times he waited for revelation when inventing an immediate answer would have been personally useful. When Aisha was falsely accused of sexual misconduct, the crisis struck both his household and his public credibility. Her detailed account says that roughly a month passed without revelation clearing her.

   > "No revelation concerning my case came to him for a month." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2661[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. During that month, he did not claim certainty he said he did not possess. He told Aisha that if she was innocent God would clear her, while if she had sinned she should repent. Only afterward did the Qur’anic passage declaring her innocence arrive.

   > "If you are innocent, God will declare your innocence; and if you committed a sin, then seek God’s forgiveness and repent." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2661[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. When his agricultural advice proved unhelpful, he distinguished prophetic teaching from ordinary technical judgment. It would have been easy to protect his prestige by pretending expertise. Instead, he told the farmers that they knew their worldly craft better.

   > "You know better about the technical affairs of your worldly life." — *Sahih Muslim* 2363[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an repeatedly places limits on Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ status. He is a messenger rather than a deity, a human rather than an angel, a recipient rather than the owner of revelation, and a mortal rather than the permanent center of the faith.

   > "Muḥammad is no more than a messenger; other messengers have gone before him. If he were to die or to be killed, would you regress into disbelief? Those who do so will not harm Allah whatsoever. And Allah will reward those who are grateful." (**Sūrat Āl ʿImrān 3:144**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَمَا مُحَمَّدٌ إِلَّا رَسُولٌ قَدْ خَلَتْ مِن قَبْلِهِ ٱلرُّسُلُ ۚ أَفَإِي۟ن مَّاتَ أَوْ قُتِلَ ٱنقَلَبْتُمْ عَلَىٰٓ أَعْقَـٰبِكُمْ ۚ وَمَن يَنقَلِبْ عَلَىٰ عَقِبَيْهِ فَلَن يَضُرَّ ٱللَّهَ شَيْـًٔا ۗ وَسَيَجْزِى ٱللَّهُ ٱلشَّـٰكِرِينَ ۝١٤٤﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ آلِ عِمۡرَانَ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ repeatedly said that he sought no payment for delivering the message. This does not by itself prove sincerity, but it counts against explanations that treat his preaching primarily as a paid personal enterprise.

   > "even though you are not asking them for a reward for this ˹Quran˺. It is only a reminder to the whole world." (**Sūrat Yūsuf 12:104**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَمَا تَسْـَٔلُهُمْ عَلَيْهِ مِنْ أَجْرٍ ۚ إِنْ هُوَ إِلَّا ذِكْرٌ لِّلْعَـٰلَمِينَ ۝١٠٤﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ يُوسُفَ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an itself appeals to the long public life he had already lived before revelation. Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ first audience was not meeting a stranger with an unverifiable past. They had decades of ordinary conduct against which to compare his new claim.

   > "Say, 'If Allah had willed, I would not have recited it to you, nor would He have made it known to you, for I had remained among you a lifetime before it. Then will you not reason?'" (**Sūrat Yūnus 10:16**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿قُل لَّوْ شَآءَ ٱللَّهُ مَا تَلَوْتُهُۥ عَلَيْكُمْ وَلَآ أَدْرَىٰكُم بِهِۦ ۖ فَقَدْ لَبِثْتُ فِيكُمْ عُمُرًا مِّن قَبْلِهِۦٓ ۚ أَفَلَا تَعْقِلُونَ ۝١٦﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ يُونُسَ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ claim cannot easily be explained as an attempt to recover a lost family throne. When Heraclius asked Abu Sufyan, who was still an opponent, whether any of Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ ancestors had been a king, he answered no.

   > Heraclius asked, "Was any of his forefathers a king?" Abu Sufyan answered, "No." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 7[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Kinship with the Prophet did not become a substitute for personal accountability before God. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ warned his own daughter Fatima that being related to him could not save her apart from her own standing before God. That is a significant restraint for a founder supposedly constructing a privileged spiritual dynasty.

   > "Fatima, daughter of Muhammad, ask me for whatever you wish of my wealth, but I cannot save you from God." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2753[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ warned his followers not to turn his grave into an object of worship. The warning came during his final illness, when a founder interested in posthumous sacral power might instead have encouraged devotion centered on his tomb. It is consistent with his earlier prohibition of exaggerated praise.

   > "God cursed the Jews and Christians for taking the graves of their prophets as places of prayer." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 1330[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. These self-limitations become more significant in light of the scale of his eventual authority. In Medina there was no higher Arabian court forcing the head of the community to live without a palace, consult others, deny his family obligatory charity, reject worshipful praise, admit technical error, or submit to a text that rebuked him. The restraints are significant precisely because political victory had removed many ordinary external constraints.[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}[41](#ref-41){: .footnote}[42](#ref-42){: .footnote}

**What this establishes:** these facts do not logically prove prophethood. They do, however, sit uneasily with the simplest cynical explanations based on money, luxury, family dynasty, easy political advancement, self-deification, or habitual lying. A stronger skeptical account must explain the same pattern through sincere religious conviction, an unusual psychological experience, or some other theory.

### <span class="num-alpha">B.</span> The Qur’an’s posture toward Prophet Muhammad ﷺ
{: #the-qurans-posture-toward-muhammad-ﷺ-is-unusual-if-he-were-simply-composing-it-for-himself }

The relationship between the Qur’an and the man who recited it is itself part of the case. The text does not simply enlarge Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ personal authority: it corrects him, restricts him, burdens him, distinguishes him from God, and presents him as bound by the revelation he delivers.

1. The Qur’an presents Prophet Muhammad ﷺ as subject to revelation rather than in control of it. When opponents asked him to produce a different recitation or alter the existing one, the response said that he had no authority to change it on his own.

   > "When Our clear revelations are recited to them, those who do not expect to meet Us say ˹to the Prophet˺, 'Bring us a different Quran or make some changes in it.' Say ˹to them˺, 'It is not for me to change it on my own. I only follow what is revealed to me. I fear, if I were to disobey my Lord, the punishment of a tremendous Day.'" (**Sūrat Yūnus 10:15**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَإِذَا تُتْلَىٰ عَلَيْهِمْ ءَايَاتُنَا بَيِّنَـٰتٍ ۙ قَالَ ٱلَّذِينَ لَا يَرْجُونَ لِقَآءَنَا ٱئْتِ بِقُرْءَانٍ غَيْرِ هَـٰذَآ أَوْ بَدِّلْهُ ۚ قُلْ مَا يَكُونُ لِىٓ أَنْ أُبَدِّلَهُۥ مِن تِلْقَآئِ نَفْسِىٓ ۖ إِنْ أَتَّبِعُ إِلَّا مَا يُوحَىٰٓ إِلَىَّ ۖ إِنِّىٓ أَخَافُ إِنْ عَصَيْتُ رَبِّى عَذَابَ يَوْمٍ عَظِيمٍ ۝١٥﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ يُونُسَ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. It publicly corrects him for turning from a blind man while giving attention to influential Meccans. Abdullah ibn Umm Maktum came seeking instruction while Prophet Muhammad ﷺ was speaking to Meccan notables, and Prophet Muhammad ﷺ frowned and turned away. The criticism became permanent scripture recited by his own followers.

   > "The Prophet frowned and turned away. Because there came to him the blind man, [interrupting]. But what would make you perceive, [O Muhammad], that perhaps he might be purified. Or be reminded and the remembrance would benefit him? As for he who thinks himself without need, To him you give attention. And not upon you [is any blame] if he will not be purified. But as for he who came to you striving [for knowledge]. While he fears [Allah], From him you are distracted." (**Sūrat ʿAbasa 80:1-10**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿ عَبَسَ وَتَوَلَّىٰٓ ۝١ أَن جَآءَهُ ٱلْأَعْمَىٰ ۝٢ وَمَا يُدْرِيكَ لَعَلَّهُۥ يَزَّكَّىٰٓ ۝٣ أَوْ يَذَّكَّرُ فَتَنفَعَهُ ٱلذِّكْرَىٰٓ ۝٤ أَمَّا مَنِ ٱسْتَغْنَىٰ ۝٥ فَأَنتَ لَهُۥ تَصَدَّىٰ ۝٦ وَمَا عَلَيْكَ أَلَّا يَزَّكَّىٰ ۝٧ وَأَمَّا مَن جَآءَكَ يَسْعَىٰ ۝٨ وَهُوَ يَخْشَىٰ ۝٩ فَأَنتَ عَنْهُ تَلَهَّىٰ ۝١٠﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ عَبَسَ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an corrects Prophet Muhammad ﷺ for being too ready to grant certain people permission.

   > "May Allah pardon you ˹O Prophet˺! Why did you give them permission ˹to stay behind˺ before those who told the truth were distinguished from those who were lying." (**Sūrat al-Tawbah 9:43**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿عَفَا ٱللَّهُ عَنكَ لِمَ أَذِنتَ لَهُمْ حَتَّىٰ يَتَبَيَّنَ لَكَ ٱلَّذِينَ صَدَقُوا۟ وَتَعْلَمَ ٱلْكَـٰذِبِينَ ۝٤٣﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ التَّوۡبَةِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. It corrects him for restricting something lawful to himself in an attempt to please members of his household.

   > "O Prophet! Why do you prohibit ˹yourself˺ from what Allah has made lawful to you, seeking to please your wives? And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful." (**Sūrat al-Taḥrīm 66:1**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿ يَـٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلنَّبِىُّ لِمَ تُحَرِّمُ مَآ أَحَلَّ ٱللَّهُ لَكَ ۖ تَبْتَغِى مَرْضَاتَ أَزْوَٰجِكَ ۚ وَٱللَّهُ غَفُورٌ رَّحِيمٌ ۝١﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ التَّحۡرِيمِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. It preserves personally uncomfortable criticism in the episode involving Zayd and Zaynab. Whatever interpretation one gives the event, a text written simply to protect Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ image had no obvious reason to preserve the statement that he feared people’s reaction.

   > "And [remember, O Muhammad], when you said to the one on whom Allah bestowed favor and you bestowed favor, 'Keep your wife and fear Allah,' while you concealed within yourself that which Allah is to disclose. And you feared the people, while Allah has more right that you fear Him. So when Zayd had no longer any need for her, We married her to you in order that there not be upon the believers any discomfort concerning the wives of their adopted sons when they no longer have need of them. And ever is the command of Allah accomplished." (**Sūrat al-Aḥzāb 33:37**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَإِذْ تَقُولُ لِلَّذِىٓ أَنْعَمَ ٱللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَأَنْعَمْتَ عَلَيْهِ أَمْسِكْ عَلَيْكَ زَوْجَكَ وَٱتَّقِ ٱللَّهَ وَتُخْفِى فِى نَفْسِكَ مَا ٱللَّهُ مُبْدِيهِ وَتَخْشَى ٱلنَّاسَ وَٱللَّهُ أَحَقُّ أَن تَخْشَىٰهُ ۖ فَلَمَّا قَضَىٰ زَيْدٌ مِّنْهَا وَطَرًا زَوَّجْنَـٰكَهَا لِكَىْ لَا يَكُونَ عَلَى ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ حَرَجٌ فِىٓ أَزْوَٰجِ أَدْعِيَآئِهِمْ إِذَا قَضَوْا۟ مِنْهُنَّ وَطَرًا ۚ وَكَانَ أَمْرُ ٱللَّهِ مَفْعُولًا ۝٣٧﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الأَحۡزَابِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. It threatens Prophet Muhammad ﷺ himself with severe punishment if he were to invent words and attribute them to God. A human author could of course write such a threat, so the verse is not self-proving. But it places the supposed author under an unusually severe condemnation of the very fraud of which a skeptic would accuse him.

   > "And if Muhammad had made up about Us some [false] sayings, We would have seized him by the right hand; Then We would have cut from him the aorta." (**Sūrat al-Ḥāqqah 69:44-46**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَلَوْ تَقَوَّلَ عَلَيْنَا بَعْضَ ٱلْأَقَاوِيلِ ۝٤٤ لَأَخَذْنَا مِنْهُ بِٱلْيَمِينِ ۝٤٥ ثُمَّ لَقَطَعْنَا مِنْهُ ٱلْوَتِينَ ۝٤٦﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الحَاقَّةِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. It warns that he himself would face multiplied punishment if he yielded to pressure and falsely attributed a compromise to God.

   > "And if We had not strengthened you, you would have almost inclined to them a little. Then [if you had], We would have made you taste double [punishment in] life and double [after] death. Then you would not find for yourself against Us a helper." (**Sūrat al-Isrāʾ 17:74-75**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَلَوْلَآ أَن ثَبَّتْنَـٰكَ لَقَدْ كِدتَّ تَرْكَنُ إِلَيْهِمْ شَيْـًٔا قَلِيلًا ۝٧٤ إِذًا لَّأَذَقْنَـٰكَ ضِعْفَ ٱلْحَيَوٰةِ وَضِعْفَ ٱلْمَمَاتِ ثُمَّ لَا تَجِدُ لَكَ عَلَيْنَا نَصِيرًا ۝٧٥﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الإِسۡرَاءِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. It corrects him over speaking too confidently about what he will do in the future without acknowledging God’s will.

   > "And never say of anything, 'Indeed, I will do that tomorrow,' Except [when adding], 'If Allah wills.' And remember your Lord when you forget [it] and say, 'Perhaps my Lord will guide me to what is nearer than this to right conduct.'" (**Sūrat al-Kahf 18:23-24**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَلَا تَقُولَنَّ لِشَا۟ىْءٍ إِنِّى فَاعِلٌ ذَٰلِكَ غَدًا ۝٢٣ إِلَّآ أَن يَشَآءَ ٱللَّهُ ۚ وَٱذْكُر رَّبَّكَ إِذَا نَسِيتَ وَقُلْ عَسَىٰٓ أَن يَهْدِيَنِ رَبِّى لِأَقْرَبَ مِنْ هَـٰذَا رَشَدًا ۝٢٤﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الكَهۡفِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. It tells him not to rush his recitation while revelation is being received. The passage portrays Prophet Muhammad ﷺ trying to retain words being delivered to him and then being told that their collection and recitation are God’s responsibility.

   > "Move not your tongue with it, [O Muhammad], to hasten with recitation of the Qur'an. Indeed, upon Us is its collection [in your heart] and [to make possible] its recitation." (**Sūrat al-Qiyāmah 75:16-17**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿لَا تُحَرِّكْ بِهِۦ لِسَانَكَ لِتَعْجَلَ بِهِۦٓ ۝١٦ إِنَّ عَلَيْنَا جَمْعَهُۥ وَقُرْءَانَهُۥ ۝١٧﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ القِيَامَةِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Another passage similarly tells him not to hurry with the Qur’an before its revelation to him is complete.

   > "So high [above all] is Allah, the Sovereign, the Truth. And, [O Muhammad], do not hasten with [recitation of] the Qur'an before its revelation is completed to you, and say, 'My Lord, increase me in knowledge.'" (**Sūrat Ṭāhā 20:114**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿فَتَعَـٰلَى ٱللَّهُ ٱلْمَلِكُ ٱلْحَقُّ ۗ وَلَا تَعْجَلْ بِٱلْقُرْءَانِ مِن قَبْلِ أَن يُقْضَىٰٓ إِلَيْكَ وَحْيُهُۥ ۖ وَقُل رَّبِّ زِدْنِى عِلْمًا ۝١١٤﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ طه</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. It says explicitly that he did not possess this scripture before it was revealed to him.

   > "And you did not recite before it any scripture, nor did you inscribe one with your right hand. Otherwise the falsifiers would have had [cause for] doubt." (**Sūrat al-ʿAnkabūt 29:48**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَمَا كُنتَ تَتْلُوا۟ مِن قَبْلِهِۦ مِن كِتَـٰبٍ وَلَا تَخُطُّهُۥ بِيَمِينِكَ ۖ إِذًا لَّٱرْتَابَ ٱلْمُبْطِلُونَ ۝٤٨﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ العَنكَبُوتِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. It presents the contents of the revealed narratives as knowledge Prophet Muhammad ﷺ did not previously possess.

   > "And thus We have revealed to you an inspiration of Our command. You did not know what is the Book or [what is] faith, but We have made it a light by which We guide whom We will of Our servants. And indeed, [O Muhammad], you guide to a straight path." (**Sūrat al-Shūrā 42:52**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَكَذَٰلِكَ أَوْحَيْنَآ إِلَيْكَ رُوحًا مِّنْ أَمْرِنَا ۚ مَا كُنتَ تَدْرِى مَا ٱلْكِتَـٰبُ وَلَا ٱلْإِيمَـٰنُ وَلَـٰكِن جَعَلْنَـٰهُ نُورًا نَّهْدِى بِهِۦ مَن نَّشَآءُ مِنْ عِبَادِنَا ۚ وَإِنَّكَ لَتَهْدِىٓ إِلَىٰ صِرَٰطٍ مُّسْتَقِيمٍ ۝٥٢﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الشُّورَىٰ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. It invites readers to examine it for contradictions. Issuing the challenge does not itself make the text divine, but it shows that consistency across the revelation is one of the tests the Qur’an asks readers to consider.

   > "Then do they not reflect upon the Qur'an? If it had been from [any] other than Allah, they would have found within it much contradiction." (**Sūrat al-Nisāʾ 4:82**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿أَفَلَا يَتَدَبَّرُونَ ٱلْقُرْءَانَ ۚ وَلَوْ كَانَ مِنْ عِندِ غَيْرِ ٱللَّهِ لَوَجَدُوا۟ فِيهِ ٱخْتِلَـٰفًا كَثِيرًا ۝٨٢﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ النِّسَاءِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. It issues a literary challenge to its first Arabic audience. The argument is not that an English translation possesses some objectively measurable magical quality. It concerns the Arabic itself: its form, rhetoric, structure, sound, compression of meaning, and the response of people competent in the language.[34](#ref-34){: .footnote}[35](#ref-35){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}[47](#ref-47){: .footnote}[48](#ref-48){: .footnote}[78](#ref-78){: .footnote}[79](#ref-79){: .footnote}

   > "And if you are in doubt about what We have sent down upon Our Servant [Muhammad], then produce a surah the like thereof and call upon your witnesses other than Allah, if you should be truthful." (**Sūrat al-Baqarah 2:23**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَإِن كُنتُمْ فِى رَيْبٍ مِّمَّا نَزَّلْنَا عَلَىٰ عَبْدِنَا فَأْتُوا۟ بِسُورَةٍ مِّن مِّثْلِهِۦ وَٱدْعُوا۟ شُهَدَآءَكُم مِّن دُونِ ٱللَّهِ إِن كُنتُمْ صَـٰدِقِينَ ۝٢٣﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ البَقَرَةِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The challenge appears at different scales, from producing one comparable chapter to confronting the recitation as a whole.

   > "Say, 'If mankind and the jinn gathered in order to produce the like of this Qur'an, they could not produce the like of it, even if they were to each other assistants.'" (**Sūrat al-Isrāʾ 17:88**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿قُل لَّئِنِ ٱجْتَمَعَتِ ٱلْإِنسُ وَٱلْجِنُّ عَلَىٰٓ أَن يَأْتُوا۟ بِمِثْلِ هَـٰذَا ٱلْقُرْءَانِ لَا يَأْتُونَ بِمِثْلِهِۦ وَلَوْ كَانَ بَعْضُهُمْ لِبَعْضٍ ظَهِيرًا ۝٨٨﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الإِسۡرَاءِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. God, not Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, is the center of the Qur’an. The text repeatedly recounts earlier prophets, corrects Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, limits his powers, and calls him a messenger. This does not prove divine authorship, but it does not read naturally as a work whose chief purpose is the human speaker’s self-glorification.

   > "Say, 'I am only a man like you, to whom has been revealed that your god is one God. So whoever would hope for the meeting with his Lord - let him do righteous work and not associate in the worship of his Lord anyone.'" (**Sūrat al-Kahf 18:110**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿قُلْ إِنَّمَآ أَنَا۠ بَشَرٌ مِّثْلُكُمْ يُوحَىٰٓ إِلَىَّ أَنَّمَآ إِلَـٰهُكُمْ إِلَـٰهٌ وَٰحِدٌ ۖ فَمَن كَانَ يَرْجُوا۟ لِقَآءَ رَبِّهِۦ فَلْيَعْمَلْ عَمَلًا صَـٰلِحًا وَلَا يُشْرِكْ بِعِبَادَةِ رَبِّهِۦٓ أَحَدًۢا ۝١١٠﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الكَهۡفِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The message places itself in continuity with Abraham, Moses, Jesus, and earlier revelation rather than introducing a new tribal deity.

   > "Say, 'We have believed in Allah and in what was revealed to us and what was revealed to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and the Descendants, and in what was given to Moses and Jesus and to the prophets from their Lord. We make no distinction between any of them, and we are Muslims [submitting] to Him.'" (**Sūrat Āl ʿImrān 3:84**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿قُلْ ءَامَنَّا بِٱللَّهِ وَمَآ أُنزِلَ عَلَيْنَا وَمَآ أُنزِلَ عَلَىٰٓ إِبْرَٰهِيمَ وَإِسْمَـٰعِيلَ وَإِسْحَـٰقَ وَيَعْقُوبَ وَٱلْأَسْبَاطِ وَمَآ أُوتِىَ مُوسَىٰ وَعِيسَىٰ وَٱلنَّبِيُّونَ مِن رَّبِّهِمْ لَا نُفَرِّقُ بَيْنَ أَحَدٍ مِّنْهُمْ وَنَحْنُ لَهُۥ مُسْلِمُونَ ۝٨٤﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ آلِ عِمۡرَانَ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. It repeatedly treats the natural world as something to observe and reason about. These verses are strongest when read on their own terms rather than forced into modern scientific terminology. Their clear intellectual claim is that the physical world is ordered, intelligible, and worthy of reflection.

   > "Indeed, in the creation of the heavens and the earth and the alternation of the night and the day are signs for those of understanding." (**Sūrat Āl ʿImrān 3:190**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿إِنَّ فِى خَلْقِ ٱلسَّمَـٰوَٰتِ وَٱلْأَرْضِ وَٱخْتِلَـٰفِ ٱلَّيْلِ وَٱلنَّهَارِ لَـَٔايَـٰتٍ لِّأُو۟لِى ٱلْأَلْبَـٰبِ ۝١٩٠﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ آلِ عِمۡرَانَ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an makes an explicit claim that God will preserve the revelation.

   > "It is certainly We Who have revealed the Reminder, and it is certainly We Who will preserve it." (**Sūrat al-Ḥijr 15:9**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿إِنَّا نَحْنُ نَزَّلْنَا ٱلذِّكْرَ وَإِنَّا لَهُۥ لَحَـٰفِظُونَ ۝٩﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الحِجۡرِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Very early manuscript evidence makes that preservation claim historically significant. The Birmingham Qur’an leaves contain portions of chapters 18–20 in an early Arabic script. Radiocarbon dating places the parchment between 568 and 645 CE with 95.4% probability, while palaeographic assessment dates the manuscript to the mid-seventh century. The carbon test dates the parchment rather than the ink, so the evidence should not be claimed to show more than it does.[47](#ref-47){: .footnote}[49](#ref-49){: .footnote}[50](#ref-50){: .footnote}[51](#ref-51){: .footnote}[52](#ref-52){: .footnote}

   > "It is certainly We Who have revealed the Reminder, and it is certainly We Who will preserve it." (**Sūrat al-Ḥijr 15:9**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿إِنَّا نَحْنُ نَزَّلْنَا ٱلذِّكْرَ وَإِنَّا لَهُۥ لَحَـٰفِظُونَ ۝٩﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الحِجۡرِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The case for Qur’anic preservation is strongest when it is stated without exaggeration. Early manuscripts, the early standard consonantal text, mass recitation, and regulated canonical reading traditions together provide a very strong transmission picture. Canonical reading variants and early differences in orthography also exist. They belong to the history of Qur’anic transmission and should be discussed openly rather than hidden.[47](#ref-47){: .footnote}[49](#ref-49){: .footnote}[50](#ref-50){: .footnote}[51](#ref-51){: .footnote}[52](#ref-52){: .footnote}

   > "Indeed, upon Us is its collection [in your heart] and [to make possible] its recitation." (**Sūrat al-Qiyāmah 75:17**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿إِنَّ عَلَيْنَا جَمْعَهُۥ وَقُرْءَانَهُۥ ۝١٧﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ القِيَامَةِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an not only forbids him to change revelation; it makes complete delivery an obligation. That removes another convenient form of authorial control: withholding material when delivering it becomes personally costly.

   > "O Messenger, announce that which has been revealed to you from your Lord, and if you do not, then you have not conveyed His message. And Allah will protect you from the people. Indeed, Allah does not guide the disbelieving people." (**Sūrat al-Māʾidah 5:67**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿۞ يَـٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلرَّسُولُ بَلِّغْ مَآ أُنزِلَ إِلَيْكَ مِن رَّبِّكَ ۖ وَإِن لَّمْ تَفْعَلْ فَمَا بَلَّغْتَ رِسَالَتَهُۥ ۚ وَٱللَّهُ يَعْصِمُكَ مِنَ ٱلنَّاسِ ۗ إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ لَا يَهْدِى ٱلْقَوْمَ ٱلْكَـٰفِرِينَ ۝٦٧﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ المَائـِدَةِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. When opponents demanded spectacular signs on their own terms, the Qur’an refused to turn Prophet Muhammad ﷺ into a supernatural performer. They asked for springs, gardens, a house of gold, angels, or a book physically brought down to them. The response was that he was a human messenger; elsewhere, the recited Book itself is presented as sufficient. He is also told to deny independent knowledge even of what will happen to him and his audience.

   > "Or you have a house of gold or you ascend into the sky. And [even then], we will not believe in your ascension until you bring down to us a book we may read." Say, "Exalted is my Lord! Was I ever but a human messenger?" (**Sūrat al-Isrāʾ 17:93**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿أَوْ يَكُونَ لَكَ بَيْتٌ مِّن زُخْرُفٍ أَوْ تَرْقَىٰ فِى ٱلسَّمَآءِ وَلَن نُّؤْمِنَ لِرُقِيِّكَ حَتَّىٰ تُنَزِّلَ عَلَيْنَا كِتَـٰبًا نَّقْرَؤُهُۥ ۗ قُلْ سُبْحَانَ رَبِّى هَلْ كُنتُ إِلَّا بَشَرًا رَّسُولًا ۝٩٣﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الإِسۡرَاءِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

   > "And is it not sufficient for them that We revealed to you the Book which is recited to them? Indeed in that is a mercy and reminder for a people who believe." (**Sūrat al-ʿAnkabūt 29:51**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿أَوَلَمْ يَكْفِهِمْ أَنَّآ أَنزَلْنَا عَلَيْكَ ٱلْكِتَـٰبَ يُتْلَىٰ عَلَيْهِمْ ۚ إِنَّ فِى ذَٰلِكَ لَرَحْمَةً وَذِكْرَىٰ لِقَوْمٍ يُؤْمِنُونَ ۝٥١﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ العَنكَبُوتِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

   > "Say, 'I am not something original among the messengers, nor do I know what will be done with me or with you. I only follow that which is revealed to me, and I am not but a clear warner.'" (**Sūrat al-Aḥqāf 46:9**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿قُلْ مَا كُنتُ بِدْعًا مِّنَ ٱلرُّسُلِ وَمَآ أَدْرِى مَا يُفْعَلُ بِى وَلَا بِكُمْ ۖ إِنْ أَتَّبِعُ إِلَّا مَا يُوحَىٰٓ إِلَىَّ وَمَآ أَنَا۠ إِلَّا نَذِيرٌ مُّبِينٌ ۝٩﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الأَحۡقَافِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an places additional burdens on Prophet Muhammad ﷺ rather than simply granting him privileges. The earliest revelations command night worship with unusual force, and classical Muslim interpretation commonly treated the night vigil as a special obligation for him while it remained voluntary for the wider community. Whatever one concludes about the exact legal classification, the text points toward additional discipline rather than easy exemption.

   > "O you who wraps himself [in clothing], Arise [to pray] the night, except for a little - Half of it - or subtract from it a little, Or add to it, and recite the Qur'an with measured recitation." (**Sūrat al-Muzzammil 73:1-4**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿ يَـٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلْمُزَّمِّلُ ۝١ قُمِ ٱلَّيْلَ إِلَّا قَلِيلًا ۝٢ نِّصْفَهُۥٓ أَوِ ٱنقُصْ مِنْهُ قَلِيلًا ۝٣ أَوْ زِدْ عَلَيْهِ وَرَتِّلِ ٱلْقُرْءَانَ تَرْتِيلًا ۝٤﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ المُزَّمِّلِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

   > "And from [part of] the night, pray with it as additional [worship] for you; it is expected that your Lord will resurrect you to a praised station." (**Sūrat al-Isrāʾ 17:79**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَمِنَ ٱلَّيْلِ فَتَهَجَّدْ بِهِۦ نَافِلَةً لَّكَ عَسَىٰٓ أَن يَبْعَثَكَ رَبُّكَ مَقَامًا مَّحْمُودًا ۝٧٩﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الإِسۡرَاءِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. It also places personal limits on him during the height of his Medinan authority. Qur’an 33:52 closes further marriages to him under the terms stated there, while an earlier passage tells his existing wives that if they prefer worldly comfort he is to release them graciously. Neither restriction is an obvious addition for a text supposedly written simply to maximize its author’s domestic options.

   > "Not lawful to you, [O Muhammad], are [any additional] women after [this], nor [is it] for you to exchange them for [other] wives, even if their beauty were to please you, except what your right hand possesses. And ever is Allah, over all things, an Observer." (**Sūrat al-Aḥzāb 33:52**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿لَّا يَحِلُّ لَكَ ٱلنِّسَآءُ مِنۢ بَعْدُ وَلَآ أَن تَبَدَّلَ بِهِنَّ مِنْ أَزْوَٰجٍ وَلَوْ أَعْجَبَكَ حُسْنُهُنَّ إِلَّا مَا مَلَكَتْ يَمِينُكَ ۗ وَكَانَ ٱللَّهُ عَلَىٰ كُلِّ شَىْءٍ رَّقِيبًا ۝٥٢﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الأَحۡزَابِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

   > "O Prophet, say to your wives, 'If you should desire the worldly life and its adornment, then come, I will provide for you and give you a gracious release. But if you should desire Allah and His Messenger and the home of the Hereafter - then indeed, Allah has prepared for the doers of good among you a great reward.'" (**Sūrat al-Aḥzāb 33:28-29**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿يَـٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلنَّبِىُّ قُل لِّأَزْوَٰجِكَ إِن كُنتُنَّ تُرِدْنَ ٱلْحَيَوٰةَ ٱلدُّنْيَا وَزِينَتَهَا فَتَعَالَيْنَ أُمَتِّعْكُنَّ وَأُسَرِّحْكُنَّ سَرَاحًا جَمِيلًا ۝٢٨ وَإِن كُنتُنَّ تُرِدْنَ ٱللَّهَ وَرَسُولَهُۥ وَٱلدَّارَ ٱلْـَٔاخِرَةَ فَإِنَّ ٱللَّهَ أَعَدَّ لِلْمُحْسِنَـٰتِ مِنكُنَّ أَجْرًا عَظِيمًا ۝٢٩﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الأَحۡزَابِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an sounds recognizably different from the ordinary speech attributed to Prophet Muhammad ﷺ. Hadith preserve conversations, jokes, legal explanations, household remarks, and political instructions, while the Qur’an maintains a markedly different diction, cadence, structure, and scriptural voice. This is not a straightforward stylometric proof, especially because many hadith were transmitted by meaning. It does, however, reinforce the observation that revelation did not simply appear as ordinary speech whenever an answer was convenient. The month-long delay during Aisha’s accusation makes the same point through timing rather than style.[34](#ref-34){: .footnote}[35](#ref-35){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}[47](#ref-47){: .footnote}[48](#ref-48){: .footnote}[79](#ref-79){: .footnote}

**What this establishes:** each point above can be given a skeptical explanation in isolation. A human author can write self-criticism, issue a challenge, or claim preservation. The cumulative question is why the text so consistently constrains and corrects the man who would supposedly be using it for his own interests, while keeping its theological focus beyond him across very different stages of his mission.

### <span class="num-alpha">C.</span> How hadith were preserved and evaluated
{: #how-the-hadith-record-was-preserved-and-tested }

Much of the evidence on this page about Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ character, conduct, predictions, and reported miracles comes through hadith. That record therefore has to be examined rather than taken for granted. The classical Muslim system for criticizing transmission is historically unusual in its scale, rigor, and detail, but it does not make every report automatically true.

Modern historians also debate how confidently later chains of transmission can recover events from Islam’s first century. Even with those cautions, the surviving material is not simply an undifferentiated collection of anonymous legends.[22](#ref-22){: .footnote}[23](#ref-23){: .footnote}[31](#ref-31){: .footnote}[32](#ref-32){: .footnote}[33](#ref-33){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}[44](#ref-44){: .footnote}[45](#ref-45){: .footnote}[46](#ref-46){: .footnote}

1. Hadith reports were normally accompanied by named *isnads* [chains of transmission]. The chain records who claimed to have received the report from whom, reaching back toward an earlier witness. This gave critics a stated history of transmission to investigate rather than leaving a saying as an anonymous maxim.

   > "The *isnad* [chain of transmission] is part of the religion. Were it not for the *isnad* [chain of transmission], whoever wished could say whatever he wished." — Abdullah ibn al-Mubarak, cited by Muslim in the introduction to *Sahih Muslim*[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[22](#ref-22){: .footnote}[23](#ref-23){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Evaluating those chains produced a vast biographical literature about the transmitters themselves. Hadith critics recorded dates, locations, teachers, students, memory, deterioration late in life, and reputations for honesty. Works such as al-Mizzi’s *Tahdhib al-Kamal*, Ibn Hajar’s later abridgments, and al-Dhahabi’s biographical writings preserve judgments on many thousands of transmitters.[24](#ref-24){: .footnote}[25](#ref-25){: .footnote}[26](#ref-26){: .footnote}[33](#ref-33){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}

1. The same critical standards could be applied even to teachers, relatives, and respected insiders. A well-known example is Ali ibn al-Madini, an important teacher of al-Bukhari, judging his own father too weak to serve as a reliable hadith authority. The purpose of *al-jarh wa-l-ta‘dil* [criticism and accreditation of transmitters] was precisely to keep affection or status from rescuing a defective chain.[22](#ref-22){: .footnote}[24](#ref-24){: .footnote}[26](#ref-26){: .footnote}[33](#ref-33){: .footnote}

1. Hadith critics did not treat every report as equally credible. They distinguished grades such as *sahih* [sound], *hasan* [good], and weak, and compiled works devoted to reports they considered fabricated. Ibn al-Jawzi’s *al-Mawdu‘at*, for example, catalogued sayings he judged forged rather than pretending that false attributions did not exist.[23](#ref-23){: .footnote}[27](#ref-27){: .footnote}[33](#ref-33){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}

1. Written transmission existed alongside oral transmission well before the major ninth-century compilations. The *Sahifa* [written collection] associated with Hammam ibn Munabbih, a student of the Companion Abu Hurayra, represents an early written hadith collection transmitted into later manuscripts. Many of its reports have parallels in Bukhari and Muslim through their own chains. The surviving manuscripts are not Hammam’s autograph, so the evidence should not be overstated, but they weigh against the idea that hadith first began to be written only in al-Bukhari’s time.[31](#ref-31){: .footnote}[32](#ref-32){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}[44](#ref-44){: .footnote}[45](#ref-45){: .footnote}[46](#ref-46){: .footnote}

1. Scholars sometimes traveled great distances to hear a report from a nearer source or investigate its transmission. The practice became known as *al-rihla fi talab al-hadith* [travel in search of hadith]. It reflects a scholarly culture in which verifying or shortening a chain could be considered worth months of travel.[22](#ref-22){: .footnote}[23](#ref-23){: .footnote}[31](#ref-31){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}

1. The canonical collections were filtered selections, not archives of everything then in circulation. Al-Bukhari knew far more reports and transmission routes than he included in his *Sahih*. Rejected material was therefore not an accidental embarrassment outside the method. Distinguishing stronger reports from weaker ones was part of the method’s purpose.[23](#ref-23){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}

1. Classical scholars distinguished ordinary reports from *mutawatir* [mass-transmitted] reports. The latter category was reserved for material transmitted through enough independent routes that deliberate coordinated fabrication was judged implausible. Most individual hadith are not *mutawatir*. That is precisely why “found in a hadith book” and “mass-transmitted” should not be treated as equivalent claims.[23](#ref-23){: .footnote}[33](#ref-33){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}

This method explains how Muslim scholarship can accept some reports, weaken others, and identify statements falsely attributed to Prophet Muhammad ﷺ. It also explains why the date of a source still matters here. The major canonical collections were compiled generations after the Prophet, so a prediction fulfilled before compilation remains open to the skeptical possibility that its wording was shaped after the event. A prediction whose canonical written source demonstrably predates the event, such as the report later connected with the 1256 Hijaz eruption, has a different evidentiary advantage.

For an academic introduction to the discipline and its modern criticisms, see Jonathan A. C. Brown, *Hadith: Muhammad’s Legacy in the Medieval and Modern World* (Oneworld, 2009; later editions).

### <span class="num-alpha">D.</span> Specific predictions and reported fulfillments
{: #specific-predictions-and-reported-fulfillments }

Predictions have evidential value only when their sources are handled carefully. They are most useful when the wording is specific, there is good reason to date it before the event, the outcome was outside the speaker’s control, and the match does not depend on stretching the language.

Qur’anic predictions can be compared with early textual witnesses. Hadith require an additional caution because the canonical collections were compiled generations after Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, even though their compilers used named transmission chains and earlier material. A skeptic can therefore argue that an individual report was shaped after the event. The examples below should not be treated as though they all have the same degree of historical independence.

<p class="theme-label" role="heading" aria-level="4" id="quranic-predictions-tied-to-public-events"><strong>Qur’anic predictions tied to public events</strong></p>

1. The Qur’an predicted a Byzantine recovery after a devastating defeat. The Eastern Roman, or Byzantine, Empire had suffered severe losses to the Sasanian Persians. The Qur’an nevertheless said that the Romans would reverse their defeat “within a few years.”[63](#ref-63){: .footnote}[64](#ref-64){: .footnote}

   > "The Byzantines have been defeated in the nearest land. But they, after their defeat, will overcome, within three to nine years. To Allah belongs the command before and after, and that day the believers will rejoice." (**Sūrat al-Rūm 30:2-4**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿غُلِبَتِ ٱلرُّومُ ۝٢ فِىٓ أَدْنَى ٱلْأَرْضِ وَهُم مِّنۢ بَعْدِ غَلَبِهِمْ سَيَغْلِبُونَ ۝٣ فِى بِضْعِ سِنِينَ ۗ لِلَّهِ ٱلْأَمْرُ مِن قَبْلُ وَمِنۢ بَعْدُ ۚ وَيَوْمَئِذٍ يَفْرَحُ ٱلْمُؤْمِنُونَ ۝٤﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الرُّومِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The historical reversal was substantial. After Persian advances that included the capture of Jerusalem in 614, Emperor Heraclius began a counteroffensive in 622. Byzantine victories followed, and the Battle of Nineveh in 627 broke Persian resistance. On the traditional Meccan dating of Qur’an 30, the recovery fits naturally within the prediction’s time frame. The strength of the argument still depends partly on how the passage is dated and on what stage of the recovery counts as “victory,” so it is better described as a strong historical correspondence than as an indisputable proof.[63](#ref-63){: .footnote}[64](#ref-64){: .footnote}

   > "But they, after their defeat, will overcome, within three to nine years. To Allah belongs the command before and after. And that day the believers will rejoice." (**Sūrat al-Rūm 30:3-4**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}; historical context: S. E. Dean, "Battle of Nineveh," 2016
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿فِىٓ أَدْنَى ٱلْأَرْضِ وَهُم مِّنۢ بَعْدِ غَلَبِهِمْ سَيَغْلِبُونَ ۝٣ فِى بِضْعِ سِنِينَ ۗ لِلَّهِ ٱلْأَمْرُ مِن قَبْلُ وَمِنۢ بَعْدُ ۚ وَيَوْمَئِذٍ يَفْرَحُ ٱلْمُؤْمِنُونَ ۝٤﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الرُّومِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. A Meccan Qur’anic passage said that the hostile “assembly” would be routed. Qur’an 54:45 says the gathered force would be defeated and turn its back. Aisha’s report in Bukhari places the adjacent verse in Mecca while she was still a young girl, well before the Battle of Badr.

   > "[Their] assembly will be defeated, and they will turn their backs [in retreat]." (**Sūrat al-Qamar 54:45**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}; Meccan setting of the adjacent verse: *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4876[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿سَيُهْزَمُ ٱلْجَمْعُ وَيُوَلُّونَ ٱلدُّبُرَ ۝٤٥﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ القَمَرِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. At Badr, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ recited this prediction before facing the Meccan force, which was then defeated. Bukhari’s account of the battle places the verse on his lips before its outcome was known.

   > He went out in armor reciting, "The assembly will be defeated, and they will turn their backs." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4875[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. At Hudaybiyyah, the Qur’an promised that the believers would enter the Sacred Mosque safely even though they had just been denied entry. The wording presents the entry as future rather than as something already accomplished.

   > "Indeed, Allah will fulfil His Messenger's vision in all truth: Allah willing, you will surely enter the Sacred Mosque, in security—˹some with˺ heads shaved and ˹others with˺ hair shortened—without fear. He knew what you did not know, so He first granted you the triumph at hand." (**Sūrat al-Fatḥ 48:27**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿لَّقَدْ صَدَقَ ٱللَّهُ رَسُولَهُ ٱلرُّءْيَا بِٱلْحَقِّ ۖ لَتَدْخُلُنَّ ٱلْمَسْجِدَ ٱلْحَرَامَ إِن شَآءَ ٱللَّهُ ءَامِنِينَ مُحَلِّقِينَ رُءُوسَكُمْ وَمُقَصِّرِينَ لَا تَخَافُونَ ۖ فَعَلِمَ مَا لَمْ تَعْلَمُوا۟ فَجَعَلَ مِن دُونِ ذَٰلِكَ فَتْحًا قَرِيبًا ۝٢٧﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الفَتۡحِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The treaty itself set the conditions for the delayed fulfillment. Quraysh refused entry that year but agreed that the Muslims could return the following year. Bukhari’s Hudaybiyyah account records both the initial frustration and the later entry.

   > The negotiator said, in effect, "We will not allow you this year... but we will allow you next year." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2731-2732[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

<p class="theme-label" role="heading" aria-level="4" id="predictions-concerning-specific-people"><strong>Predictions concerning specific people</strong></p>

7. During his final illness, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ privately told Fatima that he expected his own death and that she would be the first member of his household to follow him.

   > "I think my death has drawn near, and you will be the first of my family to follow me." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3623-3624[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. The same hadith corpus separately records that Fatima outlived him by only about six months. The fulfillment is precise within the transmitted tradition, although the surviving canonical compilation postdates both events.

   > "She remained alive for six months after the death of the Prophet." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4240-4241[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ predicted that his young grandson Hasan would one day reconcile two large Muslim groups.

   > "This son of mine is a noble leader, and perhaps God will reconcile through him two great groups of Muslims." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2704[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. The same Bukhari report also narrates the later reconciliation. In 661 Hasan reached an agreement with Muawiya and withdrew from a contest that could have prolonged a major Muslim civil war. The report is unusually self-contained because the narrator recounts both the reconciliation and the earlier saying.

   > The report describes Hasan concluding peace, then recalls Muhammad's ﷺ words that God would "reconcile through him two great groups of Muslims." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2704[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ said that Ammar ibn Yasir would be killed by a rebellious group. The statement is specific about both Ammar’s death and the moral direction of the conflict in which it would occur.

   > "Ammar will be killed by a rebellious group. Ammar will call them toward God, while they will call him toward the Fire." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2812[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Ammar was later killed while fighting on Ali’s side at the Battle of Siffin in 657. The event became a major point of interpretation because the saying was remembered within the Muslim tradition. Its value as independent prophetic evidence still depends on how early the transmission of the report can be established.[65](#ref-65){: .footnote}[66](#ref-66){: .footnote}

   > "Ammar will be killed by a rebellious group." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2812[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; historical context: Encyclopedia.com, "Ammar Bin Yasir"
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ called Umar and Uthman “two martyrs” while both men were still alive. In a report set on Mount Uhud, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ was with Abu Bakr, Umar, and Uthman and identified the last two as martyrs.

   > "Be firm, Uhud. Upon you are only a prophet, a truthful one, and two martyrs." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3675[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Both Umar and Uthman later died violently rather than from ordinary illness. Umar was assassinated in 644, and Uthman was killed during a siege of his home in 656. As with the report about Ammar, the critical question concerns the age of the saying’s transmission, not whether the later deaths occurred.[65](#ref-65){: .footnote}[66](#ref-66){: .footnote}

   > "Upon you are... two martyrs." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3675[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

<p class="theme-label" role="heading" aria-level="4" id="political-and-historical-developments"><strong>Political and historical developments</strong></p>

15. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ told Adi ibn Hatim that safe travel through Arabia, access to the Persian king’s treasures, and unusual prosperity would come; Adi remembered privately doubting the first prediction because banditry was still widespread.

   > "You will surely see a woman travel from Hira to circle the Kaaba, fearing none but God... and the treasures of Khosrau will be opened." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3595[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. In the same report, Adi says that he personally witnessed the first two events come to pass. He lived to see a woman make the journey safely and says that he himself was among those who opened the treasures of Khosrau. The narrator’s claim to have witnessed the fulfillments is notable, even though the surviving written collection is later.

   > Adi said, "I saw a woman travel from Hira... and I was among those who opened the treasures of Khosrau." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3595[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. During the difficult Tabuk period, he named his own death, the conquest of Jerusalem, and a deadly plague in a sequence of future events.

   > "Count six things before the Hour: my death, then the conquest of Jerusalem, then a widespread death among you like a disease among sheep..." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3176[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Jerusalem came under Muslim rule only a few years after his death, and the plague of Amwas struck Syria in 638–639. The plague is especially interesting because the conquest and the epidemic are separate events within the predicted sequence. Again, the hadith’s later compilation means that this is not equivalent to a modern prediction preserved in a securely dated pre-event document.[65](#ref-65){: .footnote}[67](#ref-67){: .footnote}

   > "My death, then the conquest of Jerusalem, then a widespread death among you..." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3176[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; Fordham University's sourcebook on the Plague of Amwas
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ foretold that members of his community would undertake a sea expedition and named Umm Haram among the first participants. At the time, Arabian Muslims were not a maritime imperial power.

   > He described people of his community "riding upon the sea" and told Umm Haram, "You will be among the first." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2788-2789[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. The same report later describes Umm Haram sailing during Muawiya’s period and dying after she disembarked. The prediction and its reported fulfillment are therefore preserved within the same narrative.

   > "She sailed on the sea during the caliphate of Muawiya... and after she disembarked, she fell from her riding animal and died." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2788-2789[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. During the period of Muslim weakness in Mecca, he forecast secure travel across southern Arabia. Khabbab’s report begins with persecuted believers asking for relief, making the promise of such security more striking in its original context.

   > "God will complete this matter until a traveler goes from Sana to Hadramawt fearing none but God, except a wolf for his sheep." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3612[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

<p class="theme-label" role="heading" aria-level="4" id="later-signs-and-the-importance-of-source-dating"><strong>Later signs and the importance of source dating</strong></p>

22. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ described once-poor shepherds competing in tall or magnificent buildings as a sign near the end of history. The modern skylines of the Arabian Peninsula may remind readers of the report, but the statement is broad, eschatological, and recorded after his lifetime, so the resemblance alone does not make it conclusive proof.

   > "You will see barefoot, destitute shepherds competing with one another in constructing magnificent buildings." — *Sahih Muslim* 8a[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. A report about a later extremist faction contains a highly specific physical description. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ is reported to have said that among the dissidents would be a man with one malformed arm resembling a breast or fleshy appendage. The report then says that, after fighting the group, Ali ordered the bodies searched and the described man was found.

   > One man among them would have "a hand like a woman's breast or a lump of flesh." — *Sahih Muslim* 1066g[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

   Other reports describe the same kind of violent religious faction in moral and verbal terms: they would recite the Qur’an intensely while its meaning “did not go beyond their throats” and would pass out of the religion with startling speed. Muslim historical memory identified these descriptions with the Khawarij who emerged during the first civil war. As with the physical description above, the later compilation date makes this transmitted prophetic evidence rather than a prediction preserved in a surviving pre-event manuscript.

   > See *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3610[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; also *Sahih al-Bukhari* 6930[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}.
   {: .quote }

1. This example shows why the date of the surviving source matters. A detailed match preserved only in later writing can be explained either as faithfully transmitted foreknowledge or as a tradition shaped after the event. It can contribute to a cumulative case, but it should not be presented as though a dated seventh-century manuscript of the saying had survived from before the battle.

   > Ali said during the search, "By God, I have not lied, nor was I lied to," and the body was then found according to the report. — *Sahih Muslim* 1066g[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an promised a frightened and insecure believing community that it would eventually be established in security. The promise is broader than the Byzantine prediction and is conditional on faith and righteous conduct. Even so, it is worth comparing with the community’s rapid movement from threatened survival to political security in Arabia.

   > "Allah has promised those who have believed among you and done righteous deeds that He will surely grant them succession [to authority] upon the earth just as He granted it to those before them and that He will surely establish for them [therein] their religion which He has preferred for them and that He will surely substitute for them, after their fear, security, [for] they worship Me, not associating anything with Me. But whoever disbelieves after that - then those are the defiantly disobedient." (**Sūrat al-Nūr 24:55**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَعَدَ ٱللَّهُ ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ مِنكُمْ وَعَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّـٰلِحَـٰتِ لَيَسْتَخْلِفَنَّهُمْ فِى ٱلْأَرْضِ كَمَا ٱسْتَخْلَفَ ٱلَّذِينَ مِن قَبْلِهِمْ وَلَيُمَكِّنَنَّ لَهُمْ دِينَهُمُ ٱلَّذِى ٱرْتَضَىٰ لَهُمْ وَلَيُبَدِّلَنَّهُم مِّنۢ بَعْدِ خَوْفِهِمْ أَمْنًا ۚ يَعْبُدُونَنِى لَا يُشْرِكُونَ بِى شَيْـًٔا ۚ وَمَن كَفَرَ بَعْدَ ذَٰلِكَ فَأُو۟لَـٰٓئِكَ هُمُ ٱلْفَـٰسِقُونَ ۝٥٥﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ النُّورِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an also forecast the public ascendancy of the religion in a chapter revealed around Hudaybiyyah, just after the Muslims had been kept out of Mecca. Within two years Mecca came under Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ authority, and within the first Muslim generations Islam spread far beyond Arabia. Because the language of “prevail” can be understood in more than one way, the expansion is better treated as a strong historical correspondence than as a precisely dated prediction.

   > "He is the One Who has sent His Messenger with ˹right˺ guidance and the religion of truth, making it prevail over all others. And sufficient is Allah as a Witness." (**Sūrat al-Fatḥ 48:28**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿هُوَ ٱلَّذِىٓ أَرْسَلَ رَسُولَهُۥ بِٱلْهُدَىٰ وَدِينِ ٱلْحَقِّ لِيُظْهِرَهُۥ عَلَى ٱلدِّينِ كُلِّهِۦ ۚ وَكَفَىٰ بِٱللَّهِ شَهِيدًا ۝٢٨﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الفَتۡحِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. One hadith prediction has an unusual advantage because its major written sources clearly predate the event commonly identified as its fulfillment. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ is reported to have said that before the end of history a great fire would emerge from the Hijaz, the western Arabian region containing Mecca and Medina, with light reaching as far as Busra in Syria. The prediction appears in both Bukhari and Muslim.

   > "The Hour will not come until a fire emerges from the land of the Hijaz, illuminating the necks of the camels at Busra." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 7118[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; *Sahih Muslim* 2902[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. A major volcanic eruption occurred beside Medina in 1256 CE, centuries after Bukhari and Muslim were compiled. Modern geological work confirms that the Harrat Rahat eruption produced a lava flow about 23 kilometers long and brought it within roughly 4 kilometers of Medina. Medieval Muslim accounts also describe extraordinary nighttime visibility over long distances, though those descriptions may themselves have been influenced by knowledge of the hadith. The safest independent comparison is therefore the central one: a great “fire” did emerge in the Hijaz near Medina centuries after the canonical collections containing the prediction already existed.[68](#ref-68){: .footnote}

   > "A fire emerges from the land of the Hijaz..." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 7118[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; U.S. Geological Survey, eruptive history near Al Madinah; Smithsonian Global Volcanism Program, Harrat Rahat
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an publicly named Abu Lahab, Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ fiercely hostile uncle, as a man headed for ruin and punishment while he still had years left to oppose the mission. The evidential interest is that the text commits itself publicly regarding a named living opponent rather than making only anonymous future claims. A common apologetic argument says that Abu Lahab could simply have pretended to convert and falsified the verse, but that goes too far because a false profession could have been described as hypocrisy. The narrower point is firmer: the Qur’an made a public claim about a living adversary, and Abu Lahab remained an opponent until his death.

   > "May the hands of Abu Lahab perish, and he ˹himself˺ perish. Neither his wealth nor ˹worldly˺ gains will benefit him. He will burn in a flaming Fire." (**Sūrat al-Masad 111:1-3**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿تَبَّتْ يَدَآ أَبِى لَهَبٍ وَتَبَّ ۝١ مَآ أَغْنَىٰ عَنْهُ مَالُهُۥ وَمَا كَسَبَ ۝٢ سَيَصْلَىٰ نَارًا ذَاتَ لَهَبٍ ۝٣﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ المَسَدِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. A hadith also speaks in sweeping terms about the great imperial rulers surrounding Arabia, Khosrau and Caesar. The Sasanian monarchy collapsed during the first generation of Muslim expansion. Byzantium survived for centuries but permanently lost its Near Eastern heartlands to the new polity. Because the phrase “no Caesar after him” has required interpretation, and because the hadith was canonically compiled after those early conquests, this example is less independent than the later Hijaz-fire report. It can still form part of the cumulative record, but it should be weighted accordingly.

   > "When Khosrau is ruined, there will be no Khosrau after him; and when Caesar is ruined, there will be no Caesar after him... and their treasures will be spent in God's cause." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3027[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3028[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

### <span class="num-alpha">E.</span> Reported miracles, and what kind of evidence they are
{: #reported-miracles-and-what-kind-of-evidence-they-are }

The hadith collections preserve many reports of miracles. A believer who accepts the soundness of the hadith tradition will naturally regard them as direct signs of prophethood. For a skeptical historian, they remain **testimonial evidence**: transmitted claims that extraordinary events were witnessed.

Such testimony should not be dismissed merely because the event described is miraculous, but neither should a written report be treated like a modern recording. Its weight depends on factors such as the number and independence of transmission routes, the public or private setting, the specificity of the claim, and the reliability of those who transmitted it.[18](#ref-18){: .footnote}[20](#ref-20){: .footnote}[21](#ref-21){: .footnote}[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}[39](#ref-39){: .footnote}

<p class="theme-label" role="heading" aria-level="4" id="the-quran-and-reported-public-signs"><strong>The Qur’an and reported public signs</strong></p>

1. The Qur’an was a public part of Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ mission: it was recited openly, memorized, written, challenged, and transmitted rather than being known only through a private vision.

   > "And if you are in doubt about what We have sent down upon Our Servant [Muhammad], then produce a surah the like thereof and call upon your witnesses other than Allah, if you should be truthful." (**Sūrat al-Baqarah 2:23**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَإِن كُنتُمْ فِى رَيْبٍ مِّمَّا نَزَّلْنَا عَلَىٰ عَبْدِنَا فَأْتُوا۟ بِسُورَةٍ مِّن مِّثْلِهِۦ وَٱدْعُوا۟ شُهَدَآءَكُم مِّن دُونِ ٱللَّهِ إِن كُنتُمْ صَـٰدِقِينَ ۝٢٣﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ البَقَرَةِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Water was reported to flow from between his fingers in front of a large group. Jabir’s account at Hudaybiyyah says that when the companions needed water, a small vessel was brought to Prophet Muhammad ﷺ and enough water flowed for the group to drink and wash. When asked how many people were present, Jabir said about fifteen hundred.

   > Jabir said, "I saw water flowing from between his fingers... Had we been a hundred thousand, it would have been enough for us." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3576[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

   Ibn Masud transmitted another water-multiplication report from a different journey, giving a separate companion route for the same broad kind of claim.

   > *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3579[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. A separate Hudaybiyyah report concerns a depleted well. Bukhari’s treaty narrative says that when the group complained of thirst, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ had an arrow placed in the well and water continued to spring until everyone had drunk. The two Hudaybiyyah water reports arise in distinct narrative contexts.

   > "The water continued springing up for them until they had quenched their thirst and departed." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2731-2732[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. A small meal was reported to feed roughly a thousand people during the Battle of the Trench. Jabir privately invited Prophet Muhammad ﷺ to food his household had prepared from a small goat and a measure of barley. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ invited the whole digging party. Jabir says that they ate in groups until everyone was satisfied and food still remained.

   > "They were one thousand. I swear by God, they all ate until they left it and went away, while our pot was still bubbling." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4101-4102[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; *Sahih Muslim* 2039[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. The splitting of the moon is associated explicitly with Prophet Muhammad ﷺ in both the Qur’an and hadith. Ibn Masud’s report in Bukhari says that the moon appeared divided into two parts and that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ told those present to bear witness.

   > "The Hour has drawn near and the moon was split ˹in two˺." (**Sūrat al-Qamar 54:1**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}; *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3636[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3637[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}, an Anas report that Meccans asked for a sign
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿ٱقْتَرَبَتِ ٱلسَّاعَةُ وَٱنشَقَّ ٱلْقَمَرُ ۝١﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ القَمَرِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

   The next Qur’anic verse adds an important detail: it portrays opponents as seeing a “sign” and calling it magic rather than saying that nothing had happened. This matters within the Qur’an’s own public account of the dispute, but it is not independent non-Muslim confirmation of the event.

   > "Yet, whenever they see a sign, they turn away, saying, 'Same old magic!'" (**Sūrat al-Qamar 54:2**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَإِن يَرَوْا۟ ءَايَةً يُعْرِضُوا۟ وَيَقُولُوا۟ سِحْرٌ مُّسْتَمِرٌّ ۝٢﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ القَمَرِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The moon report is transmitted miracle testimony, not external astronomical proof. There is no need to fill the absence of a securely established independent contemporary astronomical record with dubious stories from the internet. The actual evidential question is how much weight one gives the Qur’anic wording and the multiple Islamic reports.

   > Ibn Masud said, "The moon was split into two parts during the lifetime of the Prophet ﷺ... and the Prophet said, 'Bear witness.'" — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3636[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

<p class="theme-label" role="heading" aria-level="4" id="other-reported-signs"><strong>Other reported signs</strong></p>

7. Ibn Umar reported that after Prophet Muhammad ﷺ stopped leaning on a palm trunk during sermons, the trunk made a crying sound until he went to it and rubbed his hand over it.

   > "The trunk began to cry, so the Prophet ﷺ went to it and stroked it with his hand." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3583[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Another version compares the sound to a child crying and says that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ embraced the trunk until it became quiet. Variant reports preserving the same central event are one reason Muslim hadith scholars regarded the episode as widely attested.

   > "The date-palm cried like a child. The Prophet ﷺ came down and embraced it while it moaned like a child being soothed." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3584[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Ali’s severe eye condition at Khaybar was reported to heal immediately after Prophet Muhammad ﷺ prayed for him and treated his eyes. The incident is placed in a public military setting at the moment the standard was handed to Ali.

   > "He applied saliva to his eyes and prayed for him, and he recovered at once as though he had never had the ailment." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3009[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. A prayer for rain is reported through an unusually concrete sequence of events. Anas said that a man interrupted a Friday sermon to ask for rain while the sky was clear. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ prayed, clouds gathered, and heavy rain followed. The next Friday people asked for relief from excessive rain, and when he prayed for it to fall around Medina, the clouds separated.

   > "O God, around us and not upon us." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3582[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ reported that a stone in Mecca used to greet him before his prophetic mission.

   > "I know a stone in Mecca that used to greet me before I was sent as a prophet. I know it even now." — *Sahih Muslim* 2277[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }
