1. The Night Journey is claimed directly in the Qur’an. The text says that God took His servant by night from the Sacred Mosque in Mecca to the “farthest mosque,” or place of worship, traditionally identified with Jerusalem, in order to show him divine signs.

   > "Glory be to the One Who took His servant ˹Muḥammad˺ by night from the Sacred Mosque to the Farthest Mosque whose surroundings We have blessed, so that We may show him some of Our signs. Indeed, He alone is the All-Hearing, All-Seeing." (**Sūrat al-Isrāʾ 17:1**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿سُبْحَـٰنَ ٱلَّذِىٓ أَسْرَىٰ بِعَبْدِهِۦ لَيْلًا مِّنَ ٱلْمَسْجِدِ ٱلْحَرَامِ إِلَى ٱلْمَسْجِدِ ٱلْأَقْصَا ٱلَّذِى بَـٰرَكْنَا حَوْلَهُۥ لِنُرِيَهُۥ مِنْ ءَايَـٰتِنَآ ۚ إِنَّهُۥ هُوَ ٱلسَّمِيعُ ٱلْبَصِيرُ ۝١﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الإِسۡرَاءِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

   Hadith adds a detail about the public dispute: when Quraysh challenged his report, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ said that Jerusalem was displayed before him and that he described its features while looking at it. Early biography also portrays the claim as costly rather than conveniently persuasive, remembering some hearers recoiling from it while Abu Bakr accepted Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ word. The hadith report has stronger transmission than the later biographical detail, and neither amounts to independent archaeological verification. Together, however, they show that Muslim memory placed the claim into public dispute immediately.

   > "When Quraysh disbelieved me, I stood in the Hijr, and God displayed Jerusalem before me, and I began describing its signs to them while I was looking at it." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3886[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; *Sahih Muslim* 170[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}; early *Sīra* for the reactions attributed to other hearers[8](#ref-8){: .footnote}[19](#ref-19){: .footnote}[28](#ref-28){: .footnote}[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

<p class="theme-label" role="heading" aria-level="4" id="how-this-evidence-should-be-weighed"><strong>How this evidence should be weighed</strong></p>

13. Miracle reports should be evaluated separately from the moral and biographical evidence. If they are historically reliable, they support a supernatural mission directly; if a reader remains uncertain about them, the other evidence can still be assessed on its own.

   > "Nothing keeps Us from sending the ˹demanded˺ signs except that they had ˹already˺ been denied by earlier peoples. And We gave Thamûd the she-camel as a clear sign, but they wrongfully rejected it. We only send the signs as a warning." (**Sūrat al-Isrāʾ 17:59**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَمَا مَنَعَنَآ أَن نُّرْسِلَ بِٱلْـَٔايَـٰتِ إِلَّآ أَن كَذَّبَ بِهَا ٱلْأَوَّلُونَ ۚ وَءَاتَيْنَا ثَمُودَ ٱلنَّاقَةَ مُبْصِرَةً فَظَلَمُوا۟ بِهَا ۚ وَمَا نُرْسِلُ بِٱلْـَٔايَـٰتِ إِلَّا تَخْوِيفًا ۝٥٩﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الإِسۡرَاءِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

### <span class="num-alpha">F.</span> How people who encountered him responded
{: #what-happened-to-people-who-actually-encountered-him }

Conversion by itself proves little; false movements can also attract devoted followers. What makes this record more interesting is the range of people involved.

They include a wife who knew Prophet Muhammad ﷺ before his mission, a servant who lived with him for a decade, an enemy questioned by a foreign ruler, a hostile chief held as a prisoner and then released, a political negotiator who had seen imperial courts, and former enemies who later described how their judgment of him changed.[28](#ref-28){: .footnote}[29](#ref-29){: .footnote}[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}[39](#ref-39){: .footnote}[41](#ref-41){: .footnote}[42](#ref-42){: .footnote}

1. Khadija believed him before there was any worldly success to share in. Her first response to the revelation rested on the character she had already observed as his wife.

   > "By God, God will never disgrace you." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Waraqa ibn Nawfal, who was familiar with earlier scripture, understood Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ first experience within the line of biblical prophecy. According to Bukhari, he identified the messenger of revelation with the one sent to Moses and immediately predicted that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ would face opposition.

   > "This is the bearer of divine secrets whom God sent to Moses. I wish I were young when your people drive you out." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Abu Sufyan gave useful testimony while he was still Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ opponent. He rejected the prophetic claim, but when asked about specific matters of character he acknowledged that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ had not previously been accused of lying and was not known for breaking agreements.

   > Asked whether they had accused him of lying before, Abu Sufyan said, "No." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 7[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Heraclius’s reasoning is relevant without treating the emperor as an authority on prophethood. He argued from ordinary human credibility: a man who had not been known to lie about people was, in his judgment, an unlikely person suddenly to invent lies about God.

   > Heraclius said in substance: "I knew that if he did not lie about people, he would not lie about God." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 7[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Urwa ibn Masud observed Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ community as a negotiator for the opposing side at Hudaybiyyah. Urwa had seen the courts of Caesar, Khosrau, and the Negus. He returned to Quraysh saying that he had never seen followers respect a ruler as Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ companions respected him, and he urged Quraysh to accept the proposed agreement.

   > "I have visited kings... yet I have never seen any of them respected by his companions as Muhammad is respected by his companions." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 2731-2732[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Thumama ibn Uthal changed sides after Prophet Muhammad ﷺ released him without extracting payment. Thumama had been captured and held in the mosque for several days, and he offered wealth if ransom was wanted. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ ultimately ordered him released. Thumama left, washed, returned of his own choice, and declared his faith.

   > "There was no face on earth more hateful to me than yours; now your face has become the most beloved face to me." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4372[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Thumama said that his feelings had changed toward the religion and the city as well as toward Prophet Muhammad ﷺ personally. This is not miraculous proof. It is testimony to the effect of what he observed while detained and then unexpectedly released.

   > "No religion was more hateful to me than yours; now it is the most beloved religion to me." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 4372[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Amr ibn al-As remembered moving from wanting Prophet Muhammad ﷺ dead to loving and revering him deeply. Near his own death, Amr described three phases of his life. During the first, no one was more hated by him than Prophet Muhammad ﷺ; after his conversion, no one was dearer.

   > "No one became dearer to me than the Messenger of God ﷺ, and no one was more majestic in my eyes." — *Sahih Muslim* 121[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Safwan ibn Umayya likewise described his hostility turning to love through Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ generosity. The report openly acknowledges that material gifts helped produce the reconciliation. That makes the account more human and gives concrete content to the way Prophet Muhammad ﷺ treated a defeated opponent.

   > "He kept giving to me until he became the dearest of people to me, though he had been the most hateful." — *Sahih Muslim* 2313[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Abdullah ibn Salam, remembered as a learned Jewish figure in Medina, accepted Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ claim. Bukhari reports that members of his Jewish community initially described him as among their best and most learned, then disparaged him after he announced his belief in Prophet Muhammad ﷺ.

   > They first said of Abdullah, "He is the most learned among us and the best among us." He then came out and testified to Muhammad's prophethood. — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 3329[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Anas’s decade of service provides a very different kind of testimony from a dramatic conversion. He was not merely impressed by public preaching. He remembered how Prophet Muhammad ﷺ treated him when chores were forgotten and there was no audience to persuade.

   > "He never said 'uff' [an expression of irritation] to me." — *Sahih al-Bukhari* 6038[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}[13](#ref-13){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Aisha’s testimony comes from the still more intimate setting of marriage. She reports Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ service at home, avoidance of personal revenge, gentleness, tears, humor, and refusal to strike women or servants.

   > "The Messenger of God ﷺ never struck a woman or a servant." — *Sahih Muslim* 2328a[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}[14](#ref-14){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. No one witness is decisive, but together they observe Prophet Muhammad ﷺ from unusually different positions. An enemy speaks to his prior honesty. Family members describe private conduct. A servant describes everyday temperament. A negotiator comments on leadership and loyalty. Former enemies describe the effect of direct encounter. This convergence says more than the bare fact that many people followed him.

   > "And you are truly ˹a man˺ of outstanding character." (**Sūrat al-Qalam 68:4**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَإِنَّكَ لَعَلَىٰ خُلُقٍ عَظِيمٍ ۝٤﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ القَلَمِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Closer access did not produce a surviving record of private disillusionment, yet insiders also preserved uncomfortable details. Wives, servants, relatives, and lifelong companions remained deeply committed while transmitting reports of poverty, fatigue, household jealousy, grief, a mistaken agricultural judgment, Qur’anic corrections, and disagreements with him. They also preserved ordinary details of his food, sleep, speech, and domestic work. A later community creating an entirely frictionless saint could easily have omitted much of this material, which makes the witness record more substantial than simple praise literature.[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}[39](#ref-39){: .footnote}[41](#ref-41){: .footnote}[42](#ref-42){: .footnote}

1. Several people who had fiercely opposed Prophet Muhammad ﷺ later joined the Muslim community and became important participants in it. Umar is remembered as having set out against him before becoming one of his closest companions. Khalid ibn al-Walid commanded Meccan cavalry against the Muslims at Uhud before becoming a Muslim commander. Abu Sufyan led years of opposition before joining the community. Conversion does not prove prophethood, but repeated reversals among people who directly experienced both sides of the conflict belong in the cumulative human evidence.[9](#ref-9){: .footnote}[20](#ref-20){: .footnote}[25](#ref-25){: .footnote}[28](#ref-28){: .footnote}[29](#ref-29){: .footnote}[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[41](#ref-41){: .footnote}[42](#ref-42){: .footnote}

### <span class="num-alpha">G.</span> Earlier scripture and natural signs: meaningful, but easy to overstate
{: #earlier-scripture-and-natural-signs-meaningful-but-easy-to-overstate }

These arguments are best treated as secondary evidence. The page preserves them because they form part of the existing case, while retaining the cautions already attached to them: possible scriptural parallels can be debated, and claims about nature become weaker when more is read into a verse than its wording can comfortably bear.

1. The Qur’an claims continuity with earlier revelation and says that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ is described in the Torah and Gospel. For a Muslim, this naturally invites comparison with earlier scripture. For a skeptic, however, the Qur’an’s statement cannot serve as independent confirmation of its own claim.

   > "Those who follow the Messenger, the unlettered prophet, whom they find written in what they have of the Torah and the Gospel, who enjoins upon them what is right and forbids them what is wrong and makes lawful for them the good things and prohibits for them the evil and relieves them of their burden and the shackles which were upon them. So they who have believed in him, honored him, supported him and followed the light which was sent down with him - it is those who will be the successful." (**Sūrat al-Aʿrāf 7:157**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿ٱلَّذِينَ يَتَّبِعُونَ ٱلرَّسُولَ ٱلنَّبِىَّ ٱلْأُمِّىَّ ٱلَّذِى يَجِدُونَهُۥ مَكْتُوبًا عِندَهُمْ فِى ٱلتَّوْرَىٰةِ وَٱلْإِنجِيلِ يَأْمُرُهُم بِٱلْمَعْرُوفِ وَيَنْهَىٰهُمْ عَنِ ٱلْمُنكَرِ وَيُحِلُّ لَهُمُ ٱلطَّيِّبَـٰتِ وَيُحَرِّمُ عَلَيْهِمُ ٱلْخَبَـٰٓئِثَ وَيَضَعُ عَنْهُمْ إِصْرَهُمْ وَٱلْأَغْلَـٰلَ ٱلَّتِى كَانَتْ عَلَيْهِمْ ۚ فَٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ بِهِۦ وَعَزَّرُوهُ وَنَصَرُوهُ وَٱتَّبَعُوا۟ ٱلنُّورَ ٱلَّذِىٓ أُنزِلَ مَعَهُۥٓ ۙ أُو۟لَـٰٓئِكَ هُمُ ٱلْمُفْلِحُونَ ۝١٥٧﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الأَعۡرَافِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an presents Jesus as announcing a later messenger named Ahmad, a name from the same Arabic root as Prophet Muhammad ﷺ. This matters within the Qur’anic case, but it should not be presented as though the same sentence appears verbatim in the surviving canonical Gospels.

   > "And ˹remember˺ when Jesus, son of Mary, said, 'O children of Israel! I am truly Allah's messenger to you, confirming the Torah which came before me, and giving good news of a messenger after me whose name will be Aḥmad.' Yet when the Prophet came to them with clear proofs, they said, 'This is pure magic.'" (**Sūrat al-Ṣaff 61:6**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَإِذْ قَالَ عِيسَى ٱبْنُ مَرْيَمَ يَـٰبَنِىٓ إِسْرَٰٓءِيلَ إِنِّى رَسُولُ ٱللَّهِ إِلَيْكُم مُّصَدِّقًا لِّمَا بَيْنَ يَدَىَّ مِنَ ٱلتَّوْرَىٰةِ وَمُبَشِّرًۢا بِرَسُولٍ يَأْتِى مِنۢ بَعْدِى ٱسْمُهُۥٓ أَحْمَدُ ۖ فَلَمَّا جَآءَهُم بِٱلْبَيِّنَـٰتِ قَالُوا۟ هَـٰذَا سِحْرٌ مُّبِينٌ ۝٦﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الصَّفِّ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Muslim writers have long read passages such as Deuteronomy 18:18 and Isaiah 42 as possible anticipations of Prophet Muhammad ﷺ. Deuteronomy speaks of a prophet “like Moses,” while Isaiah 42 describes a servant bringing judgment or instruction to the nations and explicitly mentions the settlements of Kedar. The parallels can be suggestive, particularly within an Abrahamic reading, but Jewish and Christian interpreters understand these passages differently. They are therefore better treated as supporting correspondences than as uncontested proof.[69](#ref-69){: .footnote}[70](#ref-70){: .footnote}[71](#ref-71){: .footnote}

   > "The Messenger ˹firmly˺ believes in what has been revealed to him from his Lord, and so do the believers. They ˹all˺ believe in Allah, His angels, His Books, and His messengers. ˹They proclaim,˺ 'We make no distinction between any of His messengers.' And they say, 'We hear and obey. ˹We seek˺ Your forgiveness, our Lord! And to You ˹alone˺ is the final return.'" (**Sūrat al-Baqarah 2:285**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿ءَامَنَ ٱلرَّسُولُ بِمَآ أُنزِلَ إِلَيْهِ مِن رَّبِّهِۦ وَٱلْمُؤْمِنُونَ ۚ كُلٌّ ءَامَنَ بِٱللَّهِ وَمَلَـٰٓئِكَتِهِۦ وَكُتُبِهِۦ وَرُسُلِهِۦ لَا نُفَرِّقُ بَيْنَ أَحَدٍ مِّن رُّسُلِهِۦ ۚ وَقَالُوا۟ سَمِعْنَا وَأَطَعْنَا ۖ غُفْرَانَكَ رَبَّنَا وَإِلَيْكَ ٱلْمَصِيرُ ۝٢٨٥﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ البَقَرَةِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Identifying the Gospel’s “Paraclete” with Prophet Muhammad ﷺ requires particular care. Muslim interpreters have connected Jesus’s promised advocate in John 14–16 with Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, but the surviving Greek manuscript tradition reads *paraklētos*, “advocate/helper,” not the proposed alternative term meaning “praised one.” The case for Prophet Muhammad ﷺ does not need a textual claim stronger than the manuscripts can support.[69](#ref-69){: .footnote}[72](#ref-72){: .footnote}

   > "And do not pursue that of which you have no knowledge. Indeed, the hearing, the sight and the heart - about all those [one] will be questioned." (**Sūrat al-Isrāʾ 17:36**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَلَا تَقْفُ مَا لَيْسَ لَكَ بِهِۦ عِلْمٌ ۚ إِنَّ ٱلسَّمْعَ وَٱلْبَصَرَ وَٱلْفُؤَادَ كُلُّ أُو۟لَـٰٓئِكَ كَانَ عَنْهُ مَسْـُٔولًا ۝٣٦﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الإِسۡرَاءِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Claims of “scientific miracles” require the same restraint. The Qur’an repeatedly describes the heavens, earth, life, rain, animals, human development, and the regularities of nature as “signs.” Some verses have striking resonances with modern knowledge, and believers may reasonably find those resonances meaningful. But flexible premodern language should not be forced into the form of a modern scientific prediction and then presented as though no other linguistic reading were possible.[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}[73](#ref-73){: .footnote}

   > "We will show them Our signs in the horizons and within themselves until it becomes clear to them that it is the truth. But is it not sufficient concerning your Lord that He is, over all things, a Witness?" (**Sūrat Fuṣṣilat 41:53**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿سَنُرِيهِمْ ءَايَـٰتِنَا فِى ٱلْـَٔافَاقِ وَفِىٓ أَنفُسِهِمْ حَتَّىٰ يَتَبَيَّنَ لَهُمْ أَنَّهُ ٱلْحَقُّ ۗ أَوَلَمْ يَكْفِ بِرَبِّكَ أَنَّهُۥ عَلَىٰ كُلِّ شَىْءٍ شَهِيدٌ ۝٥٣﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ فُصِّلَتۡ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. The Qur’an’s stronger argument from nature concerns order, intelligibility, and contingency. It repeatedly asks people to observe, reason, compare, and draw conclusions rather than treating inquiry as a threat to faith.

   > "Indeed, in the creation of the heavens and the earth and the alternation of the day and night there are signs for people of reason." (**Sūrat Āl ʿImrān 3:190**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿إِنَّ فِى خَلْقِ ٱلسَّمَـٰوَٰتِ وَٱلْأَرْضِ وَٱخْتِلَـٰفِ ٱلَّيْلِ وَٱلنَّهَارِ لَـَٔايَـٰتٍ لِّأُو۟لِى ٱلْأَلْبَـٰبِ ۝١٩٠﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ آلِ عِمۡرَانَ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. Such restraint strengthens the overall case. An argument that does not depend on dubious quotations, fake NASA stories, numerology, or forced scientific readings is more credible. Prophet Muhammad ﷺ should be assessed through the strongest evidence that can genuinely be traced to his sources.

   > "And they say, 'None will enter Paradise except one who is a Jew or a Christian.' That is [merely] their wishful thinking, Say, 'Produce your proof, if you should be truthful.'" (**Sūrat al-Baqarah 2:111**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَقَالُوا۟ لَن يَدْخُلَ ٱلْجَنَّةَ إِلَّا مَن كَانَ هُودًا أَوْ نَصَـٰرَىٰ ۗ تِلْكَ أَمَانِيُّهُمْ ۗ قُلْ هَاتُوا۟ بُرْهَـٰنَكُمْ إِن كُنتُمْ صَـٰدِقِينَ ۝١١١﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ البَقَرَةِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

### <span class="num-alpha">H.</span> Recognition and appraisal outside Muslim belief
{: #recognition-and-appraisal-outside-muslim-belief }

Praise from outsiders cannot prove prophethood. A historian may admire Prophet Muhammad ﷺ while rejecting revelation, and historical influence is not inherently good. Such judgments therefore belong at the edge of the cumulative case rather than at its center. They remain worth noting because they show how some readers outside Islam have assessed the man and his career.

1. Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ historical stature is recognized far beyond Muslim devotional writing. Michael H. Hart famously ranked him first in *The 100: A Ranking of the Most Influential Persons in History*, emphasizing his combined religious and secular influence. The United States Supreme Court’s courtroom frieze also includes Prophet Muhammad ﷺ among historical lawgivers. Neither fact establishes that he was a prophet, but both show how difficult it is to treat his career as marginal to world history.[74](#ref-74){: .footnote}[75](#ref-75){: .footnote}

   > Further context: Michael H. Hart, *The 100: A Ranking of the Most Influential Persons in History*[74](#ref-74){: .footnote}; U.S. Supreme Court, "Courtroom Friezes: South and North Walls"[75](#ref-75){: .footnote}
   >
   > [The 100: A Ranking of the Most Influential Persons in History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_100:_A_Ranking_of_the_Most_Influential_Persons_in_History)
   >
   > [Prophet Muhammad Honored By the U.S. Supreme Court As One Of The Greatest Lawgivers Of The World In 1935](https://www.soundvision.com/article/prophet-muhammad-honored-by-the-us-supreme-court-as-one-of-the-greatest-lawgivers-of-the)
   >
   > [What Non-Muslims Say About Muhammad ﷺ](https://al-islam.org/articles/personalities-what-non-muslims-say-about-muhammad-prophet-islam)
   {: .quote }

1. Some non-Muslim religious thinkers have found the simple “fraud” explanation inadequate and have discussed Prophet Muhammad ﷺ in genuinely prophetic terms. The Christian theologian Keith Ward, Regius Professor of Divinity at Oxford, is one modern example: he has written that Christians can see Prophet Muhammad ﷺ "as truly inspired by God, as called to proclaim a strict monotheistic faith, and as chosen by God for that purpose," and that he can be accorded "the highest honor as a true prophet." Such judgments are not independent tests of revelation, and Ward stops short of the Muslim position that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ was the final and definitive voice of revelation, but they show that recognizing Prophet Muhammad’s ﷺ sincerity, or even some prophetic significance in him, is not logically confined to people who already identify as Muslim.[76](#ref-76){: .footnote}

   > Further discussion: Keith Ward, interview on Muhammad and Islam[76](#ref-76){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

    {% include yt-embed.html url="https://youtube.com/shorts/Zoz0q-vp7UU" title="Top Christian scholars affirm that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ was an authentic prophet of God" %}

    {% include yt-embed.html url="https://www.youtube.com/live/ftRsC_2sjg8" title="Reverend Professor Keith Ward on Blogging Theology" %}

1. W. Montgomery Watt, a major non-Muslim twentieth-century historian of early Islam, also rejected the simple impostor explanation as inadequate. His conclusion was not an acceptance of Islam, but a historian’s judgment that the theory of conscious fraud creates more explanatory problems than it resolves.[41](#ref-41){: .footnote}

   > "His readiness to undergo persecution for his beliefs, the high moral character of the men who believed in him and looked up to him as leader, and the greatness of his ultimate achievement — all argue his fundamental integrity. To suppose Muhammad an impostor raises more problems than it solves." — W. Montgomery Watt, *Muhammad at Mecca* (Oxford: Clarendon Press, 1953), p. 52[41](#ref-41){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. Muslim readers have connected Prophet Muhammad ﷺ with biblical passages about a prophet like Moses, Kedar, Jesus’s promised successor, and expectations among Arabian Jews, while other interpreters reject those identifications. These parallels are therefore better treated as material for investigation than as uncontested proof. The underlying continuity claim still matters: Prophet Muhammad ﷺ did not present the God of Abraham, Moses, and Jesus as a different tribal deity, but claimed to restore the same monotheistic line.[69](#ref-69){: .footnote}[70](#ref-70){: .footnote}[71](#ref-71){: .footnote}[72](#ref-72){: .footnote}

   > Selected comparative discussions: Jesus and Muhammad; Muhammad and Moses; the Abrahamic tradition; Muhammad and the Jews of Arabia[69](#ref-69){: .footnote}[70](#ref-70){: .footnote}[71](#ref-71){: .footnote}[72](#ref-72){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

### <span class="num-alpha">I.</span> What alternative explanations must account for
{: #what-the-main-alternative-explanations-must-account-for }

A cumulative case is only useful if the main alternatives are tested against the same evidence. Four explanations recur often enough to deserve direct examination. They are not equally strong, but none should be dismissed by a label alone; each has to account for the same life, text, and transmitted record.

1. **“He was a conscious impostor.”** This remains logically possible, but the usual motives for fraud fit important parts of the record poorly. In Mecca, the claim initially brought persecution rather than comfort, and early biography reports that offers of wealth and leadership were refused. Later authority produced neither a palace nor a hereditary treasury, worship of Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, or exemption for his family. The Qur’an corrects him, limits his claim to knowledge of the unseen, threatens him for fabrication, and sometimes leaves him waiting when an invented answer would have been useful. The eclipse on the day his son died offered an unusually convenient piece of religious theater, yet he publicly rejected the connection. A fraud hypothesis therefore has to explain repeated refusals of advantages that would have served the fraud.[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}[41](#ref-41){: .footnote}

1. **“He was sincere but psychologically mistaken.”** This explains willingness to suffer and much of the integrity evidence better than conscious fraud, and it is the stronger skeptical alternative. It still has to account for the Qur’an’s sustained literary and theological voice across roughly twenty-three years, Prophet Muhammad ﷺ’s distinction between revelation and his own mistaken agricultural judgment, the practical judgment required to lead an embattled community into a functioning polity, the striking predictions in the source record, and the reports of extraordinary events. Sincerity combined with error is a coherent hypothesis; the question is whether it explains the whole body of evidence better than prophethood does.[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[41](#ref-41){: .footnote}[47](#ref-47){: .footnote}[48](#ref-48){: .footnote}

1. **“The impressive portrait is mostly later legend.”** This is the strongest naturalistic challenge to many hadith-based details because the surviving canonical compilations postdate Prophet Muhammad ﷺ. Later development therefore cannot simply be ruled out. The hypothesis must also account for named transmission networks, early written material, competing hadith grades, catalogued forgeries, and a portrait that preserves awkward details: household disagreements, poverty, grief, mistaken farming advice, limits on judicial judgment, public Qur’anic rebukes, and a ruler who died with his armor pledged for food. Later reverence undoubtedly shaped memory; the stronger claim that it created enough of the converging record to explain the whole portrait requires evidence of its own.[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}[39](#ref-39){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}[44](#ref-44){: .footnote}[45](#ref-45){: .footnote}

1. **“The source was demonic rather than divine.”** This explanation appears especially in Christian polemic, while Meccan opponents made related accusations of possession or soothsaying. The Qur’an rejects the charge directly, and the hypothesis also has to explain why the resulting message persistently directs worship away from idols and spirits toward one God, condemns sorcery and divination, and teaches believers to treat Satan as an enemy. The hadith corpus carries the same opposition into practice.[6](#ref-6){: .footnote}[17](#ref-17){: .footnote}[43](#ref-43){: .footnote}

   > "It was not the devils who brought this ˹Quran˺ down. It is not for them ˹to do so˺, nor can they, for they are strictly barred from ˹even˺ overhearing ˹it˺." (**Sūrat ash-Shuʿarāʾ 26:210-212**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَمَا تَنَزَّلَتْ بِهِ ٱلشَّيَـٰطِينُ ۝٢١٠ وَمَا يَنۢبَغِى لَهُمْ وَمَا يَسْتَطِيعُونَ ۝٢١١ إِنَّهُمْ عَنِ ٱلسَّمْعِ لَمَعْزُولُونَ ۝٢١٢﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الشُّعَرَاءِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

   The theological objection is similar to the principle in Matthew 12:25–28 and Mark 3:23–26: an explanation in which the proposed source systematically builds a religious system directed against itself is internally strained. Unlike the fraud hypothesis, this fourth explanation also concedes an extraordinary source and then attributes it to the very kind of agency the message repeatedly teaches people to resist.

---

## <span class="num-roman">V.</span> The cumulative case
{: #what-the-whole-picture-amounts-to }

The force of the argument lies less in any isolated item than in the way the different lines of evidence reinforce one another.

1. **The teachings can be compared with the life.** The message calls for truthfulness, mercy, family duty, care for vulnerable people, honest trade, justice, restraint in conflict, humility, knowledge, consultation, and forgiveness. Family members, servants, petitioners, companions, and even opponents describe conduct that often follows the same pattern. Good character does not by itself prove revelation, but the public teaching and the reported private life are directly relevant to one another.

   > "And indeed, you are of a great moral character." (**Sūrat al-Qalam 68:4**), Saheeh International[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَإِنَّكَ لَعَلَىٰ خُلُقٍ عَظِيمٍ ۝٤﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ القَلَمِ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. **The chronology tests whether the character survived changing incentives.** Prophet Muhammad ﷺ was an orphan, worker, husband, persecuted preacher, refugee, military leader, treaty-maker, victor over Mecca, and head of a growing polity. Later power did not produce a private palace, hereditary treasury, demand for worship, or immunity for his family. The same record preserves restraint in victory, domestic work, simple living, and death without a private fortune.[8](#ref-8){: .footnote}[28](#ref-28){: .footnote}[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}[41](#ref-41){: .footnote}[42](#ref-42){: .footnote}

1. **Several episodes are particularly awkward for a cynical-fraud explanation.** He rejected the popular attempt to connect an eclipse with his son’s death, waited through Aisha’s accusation rather than producing an immediate convenient revelation, acknowledged ordinary error in agricultural advice, restricted exaggerated praise of himself, denied independent knowledge of the unseen, and delivered a Qur’an that publicly corrected and constrained him. None of these points proves revelation, but together they make a simple theory of calculated self-aggrandizement less economical.

   > "When Our clear revelations are recited to them, those who do not expect to meet Us say ˹to the Prophet˺, 'Bring us a different Quran or make some changes in it.' Say ˹to them˺, 'It is not for me to change it on my own. I only follow what is revealed to me.'" (**Sūrat Yūnus 10:15**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   >
   > <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿وَإِذَا تُتْلَىٰ عَلَيْهِمْ ءَايَاتُنَا بَيِّنَـٰتٍ ۙ قَالَ ٱلَّذِينَ لَا يَرْجُونَ لِقَآءَنَا ٱئْتِ بِقُرْءَانٍ غَيْرَ هَـٰذَآ أَوْ بَدِّلْهُ ۚ قُلْ مَا يَكُونُ لِىٓ أَنْ أُبَدِّلَهُۥ مِن تِلْقَآئِ نَفْسِىٓ ۖ إِنْ أَتَّبِعُ إِلَّا مَا يُوحَىٰٓ إِلَىَّ ۖ إِنِّىٓ أَخَافُ إِنْ عَصَيْتُ رَبِّى عَذَابَ يَوْمٍ عَظِيمٍ ۝١٥﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ يُونُسَ</span></div>
   {: .quote }

1. **Predictions and miracle reports add a different category of evidence, but they have to be weighted carefully.** Qur’anic examples such as the Byzantine recovery and the promised return to the Sacred Mosque have stronger dating arguments than predictions first preserved in later compilations. Hadith also contain forecasts concerning particular people and political developments, as well as reports of multiplied food and water, the splitting of the moon, the crying palm trunk, healing, rain, and the Night Journey. Their evidential force is not uniform: specificity, source dating, transmission, and one’s prior judgment about miracles all matter.

   > "The Romans have been defeated in a nearby land. Yet following their defeat, they will triumph within three to nine years." (**Sūrat al-Rūm 30:2-4**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
   {: .quote }

1. **The surviving portrait is not built only from enthusiastic followers describing public preaching.** It includes Khadija’s judgment at the beginning, Aisha’s and Anas’s private observations, admissions from Abu Sufyan while hostile, Urwa’s political assessment as an opposing negotiator, and striking reversals from people such as Thumama, Amr, and Safwan. None is infallible, and the transmission still has to be evaluated, but the variety of vantage points matters.

1. **The strongest skeptical alternative is therefore not the caricature of an obvious liar chasing wealth.** A more serious alternative is that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ was deeply sincere but mistaken: a religious experiencer who nevertheless delivered a remarkable scripture, exercised unusual moral and political judgment, sustained a demanding monotheistic mission across radically changing circumstances, and became the center of a tradition containing striking predictions and miracle testimony. That hypothesis is coherent. The question is whether it explains the whole pattern more naturally than prophethood does.

The conclusion presented here is comparative rather than mathematical. The case does not require every report to be equally strong, every popular apologetic argument to succeed, or every single event to function as a proof. In fact, **strong evidence is better served by refusing to exaggerate weaker evidence**. The argument rests on convergence: demanding monotheism, expansive mercy, disciplined justice, private and public consistency, costly commitment, restraint under power, a Qur’an that both authorizes and limits its messenger, historically differentiated predictions, transmitted signs, and testimony from people who encountered him from very different positions.

The human details matter alongside the large claims. The same sources remember a man who wept over his child, carried his granddaughter in prayer, listened to women, forgave personal injuries, helped at home, lived simply after acquiring power, and spent his final illness turning toward God. The portrait is therefore neither merely a list of doctrines nor merely a catalogue of marvels. It is a claim about a life in which **truthfulness without cruelty, strength without arrogance, devotion without extremism, justice without favoritism, and mercy without moral indifference** remain visible across very different circumstances.

The message ultimately directs attention beyond Prophet Muhammad ﷺ and toward God. He did not ask to be worshipped; his authority, in his own teaching, came from being God’s servant and messenger rather than from possessing divinity himself.

> "Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ 'I am only a man like you, ˹but˺ it has been revealed to me that your God is only One God. So whoever hopes for the meeting with their Lord, let them do good deeds and associate none in the worship of their Lord.'" (**Sūrat al-Kahf 18:110**), Dr. Mustafa Khattab, *The Clear Quran*[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}[11](#ref-11){: .footnote}[12](#ref-12){: .footnote}[36](#ref-36){: .footnote}
>
> <div dir="rtl" lang="ar" class="quran-arabic">﴿قُلْ إِنَّمَآ أَنَا۠ بَشَرٌ مِّثْلُكُمْ يُوحَىٰٓ إِلَىَّ أَنَّمَآ إِلَـٰهُكُمْ إِلَـٰهٌ وَٰحِدٌ ۖ فَمَن كَانَ يَرْجُوا۟ لِقَآءِ رَبِّهِۦ فَلْيَعْمَلْ عَمَلًا صَـٰلِحًا وَلَا يُشْرِكْ بِعِبَادَةِ رَبِّهِۦٓ أَحَدًۢا ۝١١٠﴾ <span class="ayah-ref">سُورَةُ الكَهۡفِ</span></div>
{: .quote }

Taken on the strongest evidence presented here, the cumulative case points to the conclusion that Prophet Muhammad ﷺ was what he claimed to be: **a true prophet of God**.

---

> *Note: These points are summaries. The cited sources support each one, but direct study of the prophetic biography and hadith literature provides far more detail than this page can include.*

### Selected primary-source index

- **Qur’an:** linked throughout by chapter and verse.[1](#ref-1){: .footnote}
- **Sahih al-Bukhari:** first revelation (3), Heraclius and Abu Sufyan (7), character and service (3560, 6038), mercy toward opponents (1269, 2937, 3231), religious difference (1312, 1356, 2620, 3166), leadership and social status (3729–3730, 4469, 7146), Hudaybiyyah (2731–2732), eclipse (1043), predictions (2704, 2788–2789, 2812, 3176, 3595, 3623–3624, 3675, 7118), miracles (3009, 3576, 3582–3584, 3636, 4101), final illness and estate (2916, 4240–4241, 4463).[2](#ref-2){: .footnote}
- **Sahih Muslim:** concern for his community (202), private conduct (2328a), attention to a vulnerable petitioner (2326), public authority as trust (1825), mercy and gentleness (2588, 2594a), repentance (2747a, 2766a), former enemies and companions (121, 2312a, 2313), prophecy and signs (8a, 1066g, 2902), animal welfare (1955a, 1957a).[3](#ref-3){: .footnote}
- **Early biography:** Ibn Ishaq as preserved by Ibn Hisham, English translation by A. Guillaume, used only where explicitly labeled, especially for the Black Stone arbitration, the Medinan charter, and the reported general clemency at the conquest of Mecca.[8](#ref-8){: .footnote}[9](#ref-9){: .footnote}[19](#ref-19){: .footnote}[28](#ref-28){: .footnote}[37](#ref-37){: .footnote}[38](#ref-38){: .footnote}

## References

The numbered citations throughout the article point to the works below. For Qur’an and hadith claims, the inline citations identify the exact verse or report, usually with a direct link. Commentary works help readers check interpretation and transmission, while modern scholarship provides source-critical and historical context. For reported miracles and prophecies, these references can establish what the textual tradition reports and, where relevant, independently date surrounding historical or natural events; they do not by themselves make a theological claim experimentally verifiable. No finite bibliography could include every work written about Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, so the list below gives the primary texts and a deliberately broad selection of Arabic and English, classical and modern scholarship actually used to make the page’s factual claims checkable.

70. <span id="ref-70"></span>Jeffrey H. Tigay, *The JPS Torah Commentary: Deuteronomy* (Jewish Publication Society, 1996). [Publisher record](https://jps.org/books/jps-torah-commentary-deuteronomy/).
